JPMorgan Chase v. Javice VIA AWESOME-BILLING

Like Benford's law, applied to Excel: “JPMC first noticed irregularities with the list when a JPMC employee observed that the list contained exactly 1,048,576 rows, the maximum permitted by Microsoft Excel.” A count that stops at the limit of a tool is a sign of truncated or made-up data. Here, the executives of a startup sold for $175 million had bought that list of customers from a marketing firm.

Bravos VIA AWESOME-GEMINI-CLI

Gemini CLI extension (14 skills, the GuardLink MCP server and a GEMINI.md context file) for continuous threat modeling as code that proves the threats: built on GuardLink, it drives Gemini through the threat model, code graph, SAST and authorized pentests (cert-x-gen, against targets you own, loopback by default) that show which exposures are exploitable, then gates PRs and keeps a retest per fix. Runs locally; your code is not sent to Bugb (the licence check contacts account.bugb.io). Commercial; needs the bravos CLI (npm install -g @bugb-technologies/bravos, Node 20+) and a Bugb account. Install: gemini extensions install https://github.com/Bugb-Technologies/bravos-plugins.

GuardLink VIA AWESOME-GEMINI-CLI

Continuous threat modeling as code for Gemini CLI: security annotations in source comments are the threat model, parsed deterministically and validated in CI, so it updates as the code changes. guardlink init --agent gemini adds its instructions to .gemini/GEMINI.md, and the MCP server lets Gemini read the threats, exposures and data flows behind a file, check its annotations, find untrusted-entry-to-sink paths with no control, and turn open exposures into cert-x-gen test templates so they can be proven. Local stdio. MIT. Install: npm install -g guardlink, then gemini mcp add guardlink guardlink mcp.

bulkhead VIA AWESOME-GEMINI-CLI

Gemini CLI extension for machines that run many agent sessions at once (several Gemini CLI, Claude Code or Codex sessions in separate worktrees of one app). A BeforeTool hook on run_shell_command blocks name-based kills of another session's processes, port collisions, git commands that would switch, stash or delete another session's worktree, whole-disk scans and long foreground sleeps, and holds heavy commands back when RAM runs low; each block tells the agent the safe command instead. No dependencies, MIT. Install with gemini extensions install https://github.com/dooly6276/claude-bulkhead.

Equivalence of non-local computation tasks beyond Clifford operations (2026) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Extends the equivalence between $f$-routing and $f$-measure (in the Hadamard basis) to $f$-measure with arbitrary bases, the controlled application of any Clifford unitary, and the controlled application of any unitary of the form $U=C_1 D C_0$ with $D$ an arbitrary diagonal unitary and $C_0$, $C_1$ Clifford circuits.

Exponential lower bounds for low-degree strategies in position-based quantum cryptography (2026) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Proposes a variant of the phase unitary protocol that needs less resources for the honest parties, proves exponential lower bounds (i.e., exponential in all resources of the honest parties) against low-degree strategies. As in the preceding paper, there is also an exponential lower bound conditioned on an unresolved Banach space geometry conjecture.

Introduces a stronger notions of position verification called quantum localization, which requires that there is a specified, unclonable state at the verified spacetime point-and that this state can be found nowhere else. Security of such schemes in proven in the classical oracle model.

Loss-Tolerant Quantum Position Verification for Metropolitan Area Networks (2026) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Introduces a QPV protocol that is fully loss-tolerant as long as all information travels at the speed of light and that has the prover prepare the quantum state. The protocol is secure in the random oracle model. It is demonstrated experimentally with off-the-shelf hardware.

Lossy-and-Constrained Extended Non-Local Games with Applications to Quantum Cryptography (2025) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Improved analyis of the BB84 protocol under loss compared to "Single-qubit loss-tolerant quantum position verification protocol secure against entangled attackers" (2023) by the same authors, introduces SDP hierarchies to compute bounds on lossy-and-constrained extended non-local games

Making existing quantum position verification protocols secure against arbitrary transmission loss (2025) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Introduces an extra commitment step into QPV and shows that for a class of protocols, involving $f$-BB84 QPV, this makes the transmission loss irrelevant for security. Argues $f$-BB84 is a practical QPV protocol.

On the equivalence between classically verifiable position verification and certified randomness (2026) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Presents a compiler that converts any single-device certified randomness protocol to classically verifiable position verification protocol which is secure in the random oracle model.

On the role of quantum communication and loss in attacks on quantum position verification (2022) VIA AWESOME-POSITION-BASED-QUANTUM-CRYPTOGRAPHY

Shows that any QPV protocol that can be broken unentangled with quantum communication, but is secure against classical communication attacks, can be transformed into one that is secure against quantum communication attacks. Also notes that if the signal loss is high enough any QPV protocol can be attacked efficiently by a trivial teleportation attack.
